## Chapter 71: Concerning the Gathering in of the [E]lements.

**¶2** The light shall be gathered in to the fire, and the fire itself gathered in to the water, and the water gathered in to the wind, and the wind gathered to the air, and the air gathered to the answer, and the answer gathered to the summons. Also, the summons to the purified Mind, which is the intellectual. The intellectual to the Pillar of Glory, [the Pillar of Glory to the First Man, the Man to the Ambassador, the Ambassador to] the aeons of greatness.

> *¶2 #1 [strong]:* The sequence is a discrete-degree “ladder” of re-integration (inside → outside): elements (fire/water/wind/air) → Answer → Summons → purified Mind/Intellectual → Pillar of Glory → First Man → Ambassador → aeons of Greatness. These are not exhortative titles but functional cosmic offices: the Pillar as axis of ascent, First Man as primal living form receiving the gathered Light, and the Ambassador as the mediating emissary who delivers it into the Great Aeons. The existing reconstruction fits the correspondential chain and preserves the impersonal, technical register of the substrate.

**¶3** They are gathered in like this; but, they will be gathered on one occasion only, and go up above to the place of rest for ever and ever.

**Assessment:** The single lacuna is tightly constrained by the required ascent-chain structure (successive receptacles of gathered Light). The supplied reading coheres with discrete degrees and the standard cosmological offices, so restoration is secure.

---

## Chapter 72: Concerning the worn and torn apart Garments, or the tattered Clothes, or the Elements, the Cross, and the rest too.

**¶7** “It is proper for you to understand this. The garments that are named ‘great garment’ are the five intellectuals that make perfect the body of the Pillar of Glory, the Perfect Man. They were purified by the coming of the Ambassador.

**¶8** “In contrast, the ones named ‘rag’: the rags are the devoured enlightening power. It is compounded and entangled in the body of the universe in the rulers who are above, who exist in all the firmaments. Also, its shine is that which lights up and is displayed in the stars of the chain and the powers of the course. And likewise, these rags that are entangled in these three earths below, above the head of the Porter [at the foundation of the earths] on the earth that is under the Porter. These are named the ‘rags’, because [for it is torn from its first garments and cast down] set [and set in the altered forms of the rulers]

> *¶8 #1 [moderate]:* The rags (devoured light) are said to be entangled in the three earths below, but still positioned relative to the Porter, who stands at the base/border of the cosmos. The substrate regularly locates mixed light ‘below’ yet ‘above the head’ of the boundary-keeper; the missing phrase most naturally gives the cosmic place: the base/foundation region where the earths are set.
> *¶8 #2 [tentative]:* The naming ‘rags’ is explained by correspondential function: light that should clothe/shine is now rent, separated from its proper integuments, and reduced to tatters through mixture and violence. This matches the next broken clause about something being ‘set’.
> *¶8 #3 [moderate]:* The reason they are ‘rags’ is not moral but ontological: the enlightening power is fixed (‘set’) into alien shapes—i.e., the rulers’ forms in firmaments/earths. This anticipates ¶15’s explicit language of ‘altered forms’ and ‘many names’.

**¶9** “However, the ‘great worn and torn apart garments’, which we have proclaimed, is this entangled living power. It is reaped, cut, and devoured. It is overpowered by the five worlds of the flesh, which creep as they move from place to place.

**¶10** “On the other hand, [these] we have termed the ‘images and the great tattered clothes’ [which we have proclaimed] are the three great garments of the wind, the fire, and the water. These the Living Spirit cloaked upon his body. With them he set in order things in the universe. With them also he emptied the vessels. He swept them out and laid them bare in front of the Porter at the foundation of all things.

> *¶10 #2 [moderate]:* The clause resumes a catalogue of names already ‘proclaimed’ (cf. ¶9 “which we have proclaimed”). The parallel phrasing is characteristic of this substrate’s classificatory style.

**¶11** “Conversely, ‘[the elements]ments’, we have called them this name, are this power that remains in all things below. It rises up in the wombs of all the earths, gathering together, and pouring forth upon all things.

> *¶11 #1 [strong]:* The sense demands the full term: the ‘elements’ are defined as the power remaining below, rising in wombs of the earths and flowing into all things—i.e., elemental mixture as the ultimate vessel of bound life/light.

**¶12** “And then, the ‘[Light] Cross’ [we have termed] this bound ‘light power’. It [is stretched out] all [things that grow] upon the earth, in the dry and the moist.

> *¶12 #3 [moderate]:* In the oldest cosmological register the Light-Cross is not primarily a salvific symbol but the ‘extension’/‘stretching’ of bound light through the cosmos, like a spanning framework holding the mixed powers in their places.
> *¶12 #4 [moderate]:* The final location phrase ‘in the dry and the moist’ points to vegetation and generative nature (dry land / moist waters). The Light-Cross as bound light ‘upon the earth’ is correspondentially in all germination and growth.

**¶13** “Also, the one named the ‘counsel of life’ is the summons and the obedience. It moves in all these powers that we have named. It awakens them, gives them ease, and drives them to their movement.

**¶14** “The one named ‘the soul that is slain’, which is killed, oppressed and murdered; it is this power of fruit, vegetables and seeds. They shall be threshed, plucked and cut down; and they give nourishment to the worlds of the flesh. Further: the wood should it be dry, and the garment should it become ragged, will perish. Again, it is [pa]rt of this entire soul that is slain and killed; since also even this whole part of it they kill [in the wombs of the fruits]

> *¶14 #2 [tentative]:* The slain soul is specified as the life-power in fruit/vegetables/seeds. The natural ‘womb’ where this power is killed and harvested is their inner generative place; this also links back to ¶11’s ‘wombs of all the earths’.

**¶15** “For all these names [that we have proclaimed] are a single [power] since the beginning, it [was one] as it enlightens; but they separated into all these parts in this first contest. They became set in all these altered forms, and these many names. Of course, if now all these varieties are laid bare, and stripped of all these appearances, and parted from all these names; they will gather together and make a single form, and a single name, unaltered and unchangeable for ever in the land of their first essence, from which they were sent forth against the enemy.”

> *¶15 #1 [moderate]:* The sentence sums up the preceding nomenclature; the substrate often closes a list with ‘these names that we have proclaimed’.
> *¶15 #2 [strong]:* What all names denote is one underlying reality: a single ‘power’ (living/enlightening power) that is later said to enlighten and to have been divided in the first contest.
> *¶15 #3 [moderate]:* The logic requires: one power ‘since the beginning’; then ‘it … as it enlightens’, before separation into parts in the first contest. ‘was one’ completes the ontological unity claim in the oldest layer.

**Assessment:** Most gaps can be constrained by the chapter’s internal correspondential structure: (1) the repeated taxonomic formula (‘we have termed/proclaimed’), (2) the cosmological placement around the Porter and the three earths, and (3) the governing thesis in ¶15 that all these named states are one enlightening power divided by the first contest. Two restorations in ¶8 and ¶14 remain tentative because the surviving context signals explanation-by-correspondence but does not uniquely fix the exact wording.

## Chapter 73: Concerning the Envy of Matter

**¶1** [The] entirety of envy is this first nature that occu[red] in the worlds of darkness.

**¶2** In this envy that [dwelt] in it, the First Man [encountered] its poiso[on] its evil-doing. His garment[s] were [torn;] it cut them and drew them; [it stripped him.]

> *¶2 #1 [moderate]:* The line is defining envy as an indwelling nature within Darkness; the oldest-layer register prefers an impersonal, ontological verb (“dwelt/was established”) rather than moral exhortation.
> *¶2 #2 [tentative]:* Correspondentially: the First Man meets the envying nature in combat; the point here is contact/assault, after which its poison and its evil-working are mentioned. “Encountered” keeps the pre-institutional, descriptive tone.
> *¶2 #3 [strong]:* Completes “poison” (its poiso[n ... ]): the envying nature has ‘poison’ as an efflux/quality; the next clause pairs it with “evil-doing.”
> *¶2 #5 [moderate]:* The garment-image in this substrate is the boundary/form of light in ultimates. Envy ‘cuts’ and ‘draws’—i.e., rends and drags away—so the intervening predicate naturally states their condition as torn/rent.
> *¶2 #6 [moderate]:* After “it cut them and drew them;” the effect is deprivation of covering/form. “Stripped” matches the correspondential sequence: cut → draw away → leave naked/uncovered (loss of ultimate form).

**¶3** ⟨p.179⟩ It envied the first image of the Ambassador, which he displayed to it in the heights abov[e], i[n] his form that it saw. It established the seal and the image of flesh, and put the impression of t[h]at image on flesh, its very own birth.

**¶4** [The th]ird time it envied Adam, the fir[st hum]an. It seized a power from him, and bore it forth from a [rib of] of Adam's [flesh].

**Assessment:** Most lacunae are trivial letter-restorations with high constraint. The substantive gaps in ¶2 can be restored only to the level of the underlying correspondential logic (envy as an indwelling dark nature; assault on the First Man through poison/evil-working; rending and removing the luminous ‘garments’ as ultimate forms). Exact verbs are less tightly constrained because multiple Coptic renderings could express the same substrate sequence.

---

## Chapter 74: Concerning the living Fire: It is present in Eight Places.

**¶2** The first place wherein it is, is this [light] land [where it is present and] exists. It is established beneath [it and is ut]terly beyond the totality. It is the foundation and ba]sis of that great earth, and the consideratio[n th]at is beneath them all.

> *¶2 #3 [moderate]:* The sense requires a predication of the first place as the locale where the fire “is and exists”; the surviving tail “ut…terly” constrains restoration to a short link phrase before “utterly beyond the totality.”

**¶3** The second place wherein it is, is the Father, the God of truth; because [the Fathe]r is established in his great, mighty consideration. So, [firs]t in the Father. The other powers that came from him were revealed through him, and (the fire) too came w[i]th them [and was revea]led by them.

**¶4** The third [place wherein it is], is the First Man, h[e] wh[who was clad with] armour. Again, it is this fire [that was swathed] on the body of the Perfect Man.

> *¶4 #3 [moderate]:* The third ‘place’ is the First Man in his descent: in the oldest cosmological register, the fire is spoken of as being ‘upon’ him as armour/garment. The surviving ‘armour’ demands a verb of clothing rather than a moral predicate.
> *¶4 #4 [moderate]:* Matches the repeated substrate formula (cf. ¶7) of fire being ‘swathed upon’ a cosmic person/body as an investiture, not a metaphor.

**¶5** [The fourth is this fir]e that was purified from all the [light] powers, [which is i]n the outer wall, in the ship of the day.

**¶6** [The fifth i]s this fire that is in the light-giver of the night [that is swathed] within the water.

> *¶6 #2 [moderate]:* Parallel to ¶7’s investiture-language: the lunar ‘light-giver of the night’ holds the fire as a bound/contained element “within the water,” i.e., within its receptive medium.

**¶7** [The] sixth is this f[i]re that is purified from the totality. The Living [Spirit] swathed it upon his body, over the summons and the obedience. He has established many things by it. He went down and stripped himself of it before the Por[t]er.

**¶8** The seventh is this living fire that is of the new earth, and is the consideration. For it is the first foundation in the new earth, it they built anew [upon it as] the first course, that is a crown for the entire earth. T[hey] are below the totality, over the entire earth of da[rkne]ss [and they have set a boundary] in it, in the [place of the] whole [outer wall of the whole structure]

> *¶8 #1 [moderate]:* The clause needs a prepositional link: the builders (powers) ‘built anew upon it as the first course’—the fire as the first foundation/course of the New Earth.
> *¶8 #4 [moderate]:* The passage describes the first course as a crown/boundary around/over the dark earth; correspondentially this is the limiting circumference that contains and orders the chaotic below.
> *¶8 #5 [tentative]:* Introduces the naming of the enclosing region; the next lacuna then supplies its technical designation.
> *¶8 #6 [moderate]:* Restores the cosmological term already used in ¶5 (‘outer wall’): the fire as the circumferential ‘crown’/wall over the dark earth, enclosing the total arrangement.

**¶9** [The] eigh[th] is the fire that shall be revealed in this [last age of the] person, and it signifies fear and war. Furthermore it [shakes the powers;] it displays and manifests its sign in [its ter]ror. It shall be r[ev]ealed in these two light ships.

> *¶9 #3 [tentative]:* Eighth place points to eschatological manifestation: the fire revealed ‘in this last age of the person’ (i.e., in the final state/phase). The surviving ‘person’ constrains the phrase to something like ‘age/state … person.’
> *¶9 #4 [tentative]:* The fire here ‘signifies fear and war’ and ‘displays…terror’; the missing verb must express agitation/assault rather than instruction or blessing, consistent with opposite-sense correspondence.

**¶10** This i[s the way] that the holy living fire exists in these [eight places].

**Assessment:** Most lacunae are small and mechanically constrained by surviving letters. The major uncertainties are in ¶8 (describing the ‘crown/outer wall’ over the dark earth) and ¶9 (the eschatological ‘eighth’ manifestation in/with a ‘person’), where multiple synonymous substrate formulations are possible; restorations there follow the chapter’s internal correspondential pattern: fire as investiture (swathing/armour), as boundary/crown (outer wall), and as fearful warlike epiphany in the final phase.

## Chapter 75: [Concerning the Letter (?)]

**¶2** Consider the summons, which was sent from the Living Spirit in the beginning. He sent it to the First Man. It is a peace letter and greeting that he wrote and sent to his brother, in which all the tidings are written down, together with everything it will bring about and establish by that summons. That summons, the First Man [... is ordained to happen.

**¶3** ⟨p.183⟩
The answer that was sent from the First Man came to the heights, to the Living Spirit. That is also a letter, which was sent from the First Man to the Living Spirit, in which all the tidings are written down; and every war and the struggles that he had waged. It did [not lack] in the answer that the First Man [set down] everything that had happened and everything [that would happen], all that had been done and whatever is made ready to be done by him. The Living Spirit knew all the wars and the dangers and the contests that the rulers had waged against the First Man.

> *¶3 #1 [moderate]:* The clause contrasts the letter-form with its total content: the answer-letter ‘did not lack’ any report. This fits the substrate’s impersonal, exhaustive ‘tidings’ register and avoids later moralizing.
> *¶3 #2 [moderate]:* After ‘in the answer that the First Man …’ the syntax demands a verb of inscription/reporting parallel to ‘in which all the tidings are written down’. ‘Set down’ preserves the correspondential sense: the inner states (wars/contests) are fixed into a stable form (letter).
> *¶3 #3 [strong]:* Retained: it matches the fixed triad ‘what has happened / what will happen / what is prepared to be done’, the substrate’s comprehensive foreknowledge schema.

**Assessment:** Most of the damage can be restored by tight parallelism: both summons and answer are ‘letters’ containing the total account of past and future effects. Exact verb choices remain somewhat open, but the required sense (no omission; complete inscription) is strongly constrained by the correspondential structure.

## Chapter 76: [Conce]rning Lord Manichaios: how he journeyed.

## Chapter 77: The Chapter of the Four Kingdoms

**¶2** ⟨p.189⟩ [The first is the kingdom of] the land of Ba[by]lon and of Persia.
The s[ec]ond is the kingdom of the Romans.
The thir[d is the k]ingdom of the Axumites.
The fourth is the kin[g]dom of Silis.
These four great kingdoms e[xi]st in the world; there is none that surpasses them.

> *¶2 #1 [strong]:* This is a simple enumerative heading introducing the four world-kingdoms; nothing in the substrate logic pushes a different phrasing here.

**Assessment:** All lacunae are either already fully constrained by the surviving letter-frames or are a standard four-kingdom enumeration; restorations are certain and require no substantive rephrasing beyond the transmitted editorial supplements.

## Chapter 78: Concerning the Four Things over which People kill each other.

**¶2** The first thing is [the obsession] of people; in that they would eat [for the swelling] of their body [and they will not be satisfied from it]

> *¶2 #1 [moderate]:* This item introduces the 4-fold natural compulsions; “obsession” fits the impersonal, diagnostic register for the first drive (food/belly).
> *¶2 #2 [moderate]:* Eating is framed as serving bodily enlargement; “swelling” maintains the negative, corporeal correspondence (inflation of the outer).
> *¶2 #3 [tentative]:* The sequence needs the standard correspondential conclusion to appetite: continual craving without satiety. This also prepares ¶8 (“leave them behind and die”).

**¶3** The second is [sexual inter]course; [and the defilement of the body] fornication [adultery] fornication [and the passions] men and women [they mingle] in it with each other.

> *¶3 #1 [strong]:* The second drive is coupling; the word-join demands “sexual inter-…course.”
> *¶3 #2 [tentative]:* After naming intercourse, the substrate typically states its corporeal/spiritual effect: pollution/mixture; keeps non-moralizing, structural language.
> *¶3 #3 [moderate]:* A missing vice-term between “fornication … fornication” is best supplied by the complementary category “adultery,” matching the list-like cadence.
> *¶3 #4 [tentative]:* A general term is expected before “men and women … with each other,” indicating the impelling forces (lusts/affections) rather than exhortation.
> *¶3 #5 [moderate]:* The clause “men and women [...] in it with each other” requires a verb of mixing/commingling, a key correspondential term for disorderly union.

**¶4** The third thing is wealth; and [the hoarding] the useless riches in [which] they shall pride themselves without [benefit] from it.

> *¶4 #1 [tentative]:* Wealth in the oldest layer is usually treated as grasping/accumulation; “hoarding” bridges to “useless riches.”
> *¶4 #3 [moderate]:* “pride themselves without … from it” calls for “without benefit/profit,” i.e., no real use (correspondence: riches without use are empty externals).

**¶5** The fourth is war; and the disputes, the [judgments] and the trials that they instigate against each other. Someone is victor over another, with each other in tyranny, so that they have received a name in the world.

> *¶5 #1 [tentative]:* “the disputes, the … and the trials” fits a triad of conflict-forms; “judgments” (legal contests) pairs naturally with disputes and trials.

**¶6** All people, they who side with these four major things that [a]re on every tongue [in the] world [and in them] they struggle so that they might generate them [desires] among them.

> *¶6 #3 [tentative]:* The sentence needs a connective to continue: people strive to generate these things “in the world … and in them/among them.” This keeps the impersonal observational tone.
> *¶6 #4 [tentative]:* They “struggle so that they might generate them … among them”: the generated products are the inner impulses that then become outer acts; “desires” matches the correspondential inside→outside logic.

**¶7** [When the Living Spirit comes,] he separates the Light from the Darkness, the good from the wicked;

> *¶7 #1 [moderate]:* The subject must be a cosmic separator who divides Light/Darkness. In the Iranian-Manichaean substrate this role belongs to the Living Spirit (organizing/separating power), stated without apostolic frame.

**¶8** For they will not remain with them for ete[rni]ty; rather they shall leave them behind and die.

**Assessment:** Most gaps can be restored at the level of correspondential function (four compulsions; insatiability; mixture/defilement; useless wealth; conflict triad; cosmic separator). Exact lexical choices for the smaller list-terms (¶3–¶6) remain somewhat underconstrained without additional manuscript traces, so several restorations are necessarily tentative but consistent with the oldest cosmological register and inside→outside logic.

---

## Chapter 79: Concerning the Fasting of the Saints

**¶3** The first work: Shall the holy man punish his body by fasting, he subdues the entire ruling-power that exists in him.

**¶4** The second: This soul that comes into him in the administration of his food, day by day; it shall be made holy, cleansed, purified, and washed from the adulteration of the Darkness that is mixed in with it.

**¶5** The third: That person shall make every deed a holy one; the mystery of [the children] of light in whom there is neither corruption nor [defilement in] the food, nor wound it. Rather, they are holy, [there is nothing] in them that defiles, as they live in peace.

> *¶5 #1 [strong]:* Fixed technical phrase in this stratum: “children of Light” names the unmixed Light-nature (pure members/particles) contrasted with the adulterated mixture in food.
> *¶5 #2 [moderate]:* The clause requires a second negated predicate parallel to “neither corruption nor …”; correspondentially, food is an ultimate plane where mixture can either corrupt or defile. “Defilement” matches the later sentence “there is nothing in them that defiles” and keeps the impersonal cosmological register (not moralizing).
> *¶5 #3 [strong]:* Anticipates and grounds the following “that defiles”; expresses the Light-substance as without alien admixture.

**¶6** The fourth: They make a [seal upon] the Cross, they restrain their hands from the hand [that they may] destroy the living soul.

> *¶6 #1 [moderate]:* In the oldest correspondential idiom, “to make a seal upon the Cross” = to set a boundary/mark of restraint at the intersection where powers act into ultimates (hands/works). This fits the next member about restraining the hands.
> *¶6 #2 [strong]:* Syntax demands a purposive connector before “destroy the living soul.” Restoring “that they may not destroy the living soul” preserves the causal logic: restraint of hands prevents harm to Life/Soul (the living Light in creatures).

**Assessment:** Most gaps are locally constrained by parallelism and the correspondential chain (Light’s incorruptibility → no defilement in food → harmless action). ¶6’s restorations are moderately constrained by the ethical-cosmological logic of restraint protecting the living soul, though the exact noun phrase before “the Cross” could admit a small set of near equivalents (e.g., ‘seal’/‘sign’).

---

## Chapter 80: The Chapter of the Commandments of Righteousness

---

## Chapter 81: The Chapter of Fasting, for it engenders a Host of Angels

---

## Chapter 82: The Chapter of Righteous [Judgement]

**¶2** Through anger, then, I will [break him] before him. That person shall wither and be subdued instantly and come to the place [of judgement] with righteous judgement.

> *¶2 #1 [moderate]:* The logic is disciplinary, not punitive: anger as a force that ‘breaks/subdues’ the resistant will so it can be brought under order and judgement.
> *¶2 #2 [moderate]:* The sentence already ends “with righteous judgement”; the missing phrase is the corresponding ‘place’—i.e., the ordered locus/state where judgement is executed.

**¶4** "[Concerning] this anger with which you shall be angry, you are making in it a judgement of righteousness [when you make] difficult people [against] repentance. Their anger and wickedness and evil-doing [so that they may come to] is only a scare and a rebuke; because [in them] For the anger alone is the scare, and when [the fear of judgement is set over them] you would censure and upbraid that brother with words that scare, you are shaming him to good use, so that you might strip him of the obduracy [the anger ceases] in this [that is in him] his heart [is softened]."

> *¶4 #1 [moderate]:* The paragraph opens with a definition/classification of ‘this anger’ (the remedial kind). A heading-like particle fits the substrate’s impersonal didactic style.
> *¶4 #2 [moderate]:* Sets up anger as an act of discernment/judgement rather than passion; fits the following ‘you are making in it a judgement of righteousness’.
> *¶4 #3 [strong]:* ‘Angry against’ difficult people; the construction is constrained by the next clause about their repentance.
> *¶4 #4 [moderate]:* Anger-as-rebuke aims at turning (repentance) rather than retaliation; this supplies the purpose clause.
> *¶4 #5 [moderate]:* Refers back to ‘Their anger and wickedness…’; the evil is internal state, while the rebuke is an external corrective.
> *¶4 #6 [tentative]:* Correspondentially, fear is an outer restraint that checks evil until the inner can be turned; matches ‘scare and rebuke’.
> *¶4 #7 [moderate]:* The ‘scare’ is not perpetual; once its work is done it withdraws—consistent with anger as medicinal stimulus.
> *¶4 #8 [moderate]:* Obduracy is located in the person’s heart/will; needed to connect to the following ‘in this … his heart’.
> *¶4 #9 [moderate]:* Matches the sequence: strip obduracy → in this way → heart changes. ‘Softened’ is the natural correspondence of a will turned from hard resistance.

**¶5** "However, you have heard [of the other kind of] the anger. For this is the anger that lets you find occasion to lust after an evil thing with sin in it. [And if you] listen to him [If you] and he is angry with an anger [that is fierce] look at him [again] and he is violent [but only] he does not utter constructive words [mock] composed anger; he is angry in his pride and boastfulness. They speak ill in that they would [because of] him. And they do not honour him as he is angry and abuses and curses, and wishes to humiliate the meek, in retaliation and violence against his ill-treated brothers. He engenders the things that [stir up hatred] or else if someone will falter and commit sin [through it] and does not accept judgement. He is angry or he walks in his own [own counsel] not of the opinion of his brothers. They reprimand him about it, and he assumes anger and commits an evil deed in his anger. This is the evil anger [of bitterness] and zeal. [Some] call it anger."

> *¶5 #1 [moderate]:* Contrasts the remedial anger of ¶4 with the destructive anger now described; the didactic structure demands an explicit contrast-marker.
> *¶5 #2 [moderate]:* Introduces a conditional example leading into the chain of behaviours (listening, looking, violence).
> *¶5 #3 [moderate]:* Parallel to the previous conditional; keeps the rhythm of exempla.
> *¶5 #4 [moderate]:* Opposed to ‘composed anger’ later; ‘fierce’ captures the unruled, inflamed quality of the evil form.
> *¶5 #5 [moderate]:* A repeated observational cue (‘look at him … and …’).
> *¶5 #6 [moderate]:* Sets antithesis: he does not utter constructive words, but only destructive/abusive speech.
> *¶5 #7 [moderate]:* ‘They speak ill …’ aims at derision; in the substrate this is the speech-correspondence of contempt.
> *¶5 #8 [strong]:* Causal link: ill speech arises ‘because of’ his anger/abuses; tight syntactic necessity.
> *¶5 #9 [moderate]:* What this anger ‘engenders’ is not merely acts but affections; hatred is the inner heat corresponding to violent speech and retaliation.
> *¶5 #10 [moderate]:* Sin is committed ‘through’ the opened occasion provided by anger; connects mechanism to effect.
> *¶5 #11 [moderate]:* Walking in one’s own counsel/opinion corresponds to self-derived intelligence (pride) opposed to concord with ‘brothers’.
> *¶5 #12 [tentative]:* Evil anger is paired with zeal in its dark form; ‘bitterness’ is the affective correspondence of rancorous zeal.
> *¶5 #13 [moderate]:* Generic classifier: ‘Some call it anger’—the teacher then corrects/defines it.

**¶6** "[He said:] Whenever you may produce evil or [good] two or one [persons] becomes obdurate and not listen to you. You see him [and] he humiliates himself, assuming up to now a [softness] You understand that they [are not showing] harshness to you [and you answer] them with hard words, in cruelty and harshness. And they will not be able to name it anger; rather its name is the strong reprimand that leads to repentance.

> *¶6 #1 [tentative]:* Introduces the next impersonal rule/example; even in the stripped layer a minimal speech-intro often remains.
> *¶6 #2 [moderate]:* The pair is ‘evil or good’ as two modes of producing/using anger/rebuke; corresponds to opposite senses of the same appearance.
> *¶6 #3 [strong]:* ‘two or one persons’ as recipients of rebuke; grammatical completion.
> *¶6 #4 [strong]:* Joins the observation chain: ‘You see him … and he humiliates himself…’.
> *¶6 #5 [moderate]:* The humbled one has assumed ‘softness’ (yielding) rather than hardness; corresponds to receptive state.
> *¶6 #6 [moderate]:* You understand ‘they are not showing harshness to you’; keeps the distinction between corrective severity and actual harshness/cruelty.
> *¶6 #7 [moderate]:* Completes the reciprocal scenario: if you respond with hard words, that would be cruelty; sets up the naming distinction.

**¶7** For you are like a wise doctor, whose hand is sensitive in [touching] the wounds that he shall heal are severe [and] sometimes he shall place on the wound a remedy that is hot, sometimes a remedy that is cool [; and] after the blow. Corresponding to the simile [and] this too is how it happens [of the doctor] There are some, if he shall take the rebuke [with those who are being corrected]

> *¶7 #1 [strong]:* Doctor’s ‘hand’ is sensitive in touching/handling wounds; correspondence: discernment applied to states.
> *¶7 #2 [strong]:* Joins severity of wounds to the consequent variety of remedies.
> *¶7 #3 [moderate]:* Punctuates the alternation (hot/cool) as ordered procedure rather than passion.
> *¶7 #4 [strong]:* Connects the final clause ‘after the blow’ with the preceding treatment; stylistic connective.
> *¶7 #5 [moderate]:* Makes explicit the referent of the simile before applying it; didactic clarity typical of the substrate.
> *¶7 #6 [tentative]:* Applies the medical correspondence to instruction/rebuke: the healer’s varied remedies correspond to varied words/states.

**¶8** Through the hard word [is like] the blow that shall be cured by the remedy [; there are some] of people if he shall take the rebuke [and yet others] with the humbling word, just as this [one is] healed by the cool remedy [also] the wise person is like this understanding doctor [who] speaks to him as he is subdued corresponding to error [in two ways] in humility. Also, this one who will speak to him [he may make them become] he spoke [he discerns] so that [one with] they of godly opinion. And [because of] so that he might [when he sees] the brother in two aspects [or] love, but the other one with fear [as a restraint] obduracy in one of his brothers [until he ceases] foolishness. He may speak with those ones with great fear [if he sees] of evil-doing and hate. Fear, however, leads to repentance [meek] Yet, this one [fitting] as even he is [of love] he reckons words and what is [gently] in the aspect [with order] and he upbraids that brother until he brings him to repentance.

> *¶8 #1 [moderate]:* Resumes the simile: hard word corresponds to hot remedy/blow; ‘is like’ signals correspondence mapping.
> *¶8 #2 [moderate]:* Introduces the second class of recipients (those helped by cool/humbling word).
> *¶8 #3 [moderate]:* Balances the two types: some take hard rebuke, others take gentle rebuke.
> *¶8 #4 [strong]:* ‘just as this one is healed…’—required for the comparison.
> *¶8 #5 [strong]:* Marks continuation from the simile to the rule for the wise person.
> *¶8 #6 [strong]:* ‘this one who will speak to him’—relative pronoun completion.
> *¶8 #7 [strong]:* The whole unit turns on the dual-aspect doctrine (love/fear); this phrase sets up ‘two aspects’.
> *¶8 #8 [moderate]:* Purpose of speaking: to form them into right-mindedness (‘godly opinion’ here as order/truth), not to vent emotion.
> *¶8 #9 [moderate]:* Needed before ‘so that he might … the brother in two aspects’; discernment corresponds to the ‘sensitive hand’ of the doctor.
> *¶8 #10 [strong]:* Grammatically required: ‘one with love, but the other…’.
> *¶8 #11 [strong]:* Fear is applied ‘because of’ obduracy/foolishness; causal connector.
> *¶8 #12 [moderate]:* Introduces the condition for using fear: when obduracy appears.
> *¶8 #13 [strong]:* Pairs ‘obduracy … or foolishness’ as two resistant states.
> *¶8 #14 [moderate]:* Fear functions correspondentially as an external restraint on evil until internal turning occurs.
> *¶8 #15 [tentative]:* Fear continues until evil-doing/hate ceases; matches the medicinal, temporary role of fear.
> *¶8 #16 [moderate]:* Introduces the other case: even if the brother is meek/subdued, words are still weighed.
> *¶8 #17 [moderate]:* Opposite of obdurate: a yielded, receptive state.
> *¶8 #18 [moderate]:* He ‘reckons words and what is fitting’—discernment of appropriate speech/remedy.
> *¶8 #19 [strong]:* Completes ‘in the aspect …’; the next aspect is fear, so this must be love.
> *¶8 #20 [moderate]:* Upbraids ‘gently’ until repentance: love-aspect correction is mild yet real; corresponds to the cool remedy.
> *¶8 #21 [tentative]:* Repentance here is return to order/measure, not devotional contrition; fits oldest substrate register and the chapter’s concern with regulated, two-aspect governance.

**¶9** For the light-givers that are in the heavens shall be revealed with these two aspects, in the aspect of love and the aspect of fear. They display these two aspects of the powers above that are in the heavens, because of their soul that passes through the totality. For in these two appearances, the aspect of love that is beauty and sublimity, together with the other aspect of confrontation that is in them, they shall purify in them the powers above.

**Assessment:** Most gaps are recoverable at the level of structural correspondences: anger divides into two discrete uses (medicinal fear vs destructive heat), mapped by the doctor/hot-cool remedy simile and then lifted to the cosmic dual-aspect of the light-givers (love/beauty vs confrontation/fear). Exact particles and some abstract nouns (esp. ¶4 #6, ¶5 #12, ¶8 #14-15, ¶8 #21) remain underconstrained, so those restorations are tentative, but the required dual-aspect logic tightly constrains the overall content.

---

## Chapter 83: Concerning the Man who is ugly in his Body, [but] beautiful [in his Soul].

**¶9** “Pearls shall arise not in every place in the sea, nor be formed in the sea as a whole. Rather, in various places that are in this sea, pearls are formed in them [for it is only in] that sea, in which the [pearls] shall be formed [within] this [deep basin of] what the sea shall [receives and bears] its fire {blazes} above and comes [down from the height] and it makes foam like the drop of water that flows [from above] down in rainwater [and gathers] is the water [that comes]own first [as a fine] foam and comes down [upon the surface] the sweet waters [above all] the waters.

> *¶9 #1 [moderate]:* Construes the sentence: pearls form in specific places *in* the sea; the next clause contrasts “in various places” with “in which the pearls shall be formed.” The substrate register prefers impersonal causal linkage (“for it is only in … that …”).
> *¶9 #3 [moderate]:* Requires a locative completing “in which the pearls shall be formed within this …”.
> *¶9 #4 [tentative]:* The correspondence is the receptive hollow (basin) in the sea where the conditions collect; “basin” matches the shell/containment logic that governs the whole similitude.
> *¶9 #5 [tentative]:* The sea is treated as a vessel that receives the descent (fire/heat → vapor → drop). “Receives and bears” is the mechanical, non-exhortative substrate wording.
> *¶9 #6 [moderate]:* Fire/heat rises above, then “comes down” as effect (condensation/rain). Correspondentially: influx from above into lower waters.
> *¶9 #7 [moderate]:* Completes “flows … down in rainwater”: the drop’s origin is the upper region.
> *¶9 #8 [tentative]:* Needed to join the process: drop → gathers → descends; matches formation-by-collection before descent.
> *¶9 #9 [moderate]:* Restores “is the water that comes down first …”: identifies the first-descending water that produces foam.
> *¶9 #10 [tentative]:* Prepares “foam” as a texture/state produced by descent and agitation; “fine foam” is a naturalistic description consistent with the simile.
> *¶9 #11 [strong]:* Foam forms on/at the surface; this anticipates ¶10’s explicit “floated on the surface of the waters.”
> *¶9 #12 [tentative]:* Balances “the sweet waters … the waters”: sweet waters are above the bitter/deep waters; correspondence: purer receptacles are higher.

**¶10** “This drop of water shall [descend] down to the sweet waters and [entered into them] and they absorbed them and were combined with the [foam]. They did not [d]escend to the depths of the sea, but they [remained above] it floated on the surface of the waters [until] to it. The foam and the pearl-shell shall be formed [and is near] this wholesome drop [from] it, and it becomes a pe[arl] that makes a drop of rainwater [upon] waters [and] it not being whole. It breaks and separates out into many droplets, and it has time to become a drop of sweet water [because] and comes up in the sea of [again] rain and sweet water; and it is accomodated in the shell, which at first is foam. They shall be joined with each other at [this] time, and are shaped and become a great pea[r]l, a great and valued kind.

> *¶10 #1 [strong]:* Directly required by “shall ___ down to the sweet waters.”
> *¶10 #2 [moderate]:* The drop “descends … and entered … and they absorbed them”: impersonal physical assimilation mirrors spiritual reception.
> *¶10 #3 [moderate]:* They are “combined with the ___”; the only coherent referent is the foam-state forming from drop + sea-water agitation, which then becomes shell.
> *¶10 #5 [strong]:* Opposition: not descending to depths, but remaining above/surface.
> *¶10 #6 [tentative]:* Bridges to the next clause “it floated on the surface …”: temporal connection while it remains above.
> *¶10 #7 [tentative]:* Completes “it floated on the surface of the waters … to it”: proximity of drop/foam to the surface where shells form.
> *¶10 #8 [strong]:* “shall be formed ___ this wholesome drop”: formed *from* the wholesome drop.
> *¶10 #10 [moderate]:* “drop of rainwater ___ waters”: standard phrasing “upon the waters.”
> *¶10 #11 [strong]:* Connective required: “waters … it not being whole.”
> *¶10 #12 [tentative]:* Introduces the reason for breaking/separating: “and it not being whole. It breaks …”.
> *¶10 #13 [tentative]:* “has time to become a drop of sweet water … and comes up”: cyclical reforming into a single drop again.

**¶11** “When, however, a drop of rainwater falls, and that drop breaks into many droplets and various {particles of water}, they shall be formed into and be confined in [n]umerous pearls, in the shell and the pearl-shell. One might form two pearls, another may form three, others may form five; some mould more than these, some fewer.

**¶12** “Now, when you might [find a] whole drop, and the shell receives it, it shall become a great and valued [pe]arl as its worth is perfected. [However], if these two droplets will have time (to adhere) to one another before any (water particle} escapes, and they mix with e[ach o]ther, and the shell [receives them] before they break into [many fragments] within [the shell] in a great kind [and they are shaped] the drop of rain, which [and becomes joined to] another one, that [does not let go of] the [w]aters form them in [and thus] in a great, valued commodity.

> *¶12 #5 [moderate]:* The shell is the receptacle; it must “receive” the adhering droplets before loss/escape.
> *¶12 #6 [moderate]:* Parallel to ¶11: breaking into many droplets/particles; here: “before they break into …”.
> *¶12 #7 [moderate]:* Restores “within ___ in a great kind”: locus of formation is within the shell.
> *¶12 #8 [moderate]:* Matches ¶10: “are shaped and become a great pearl”; the same forming-verb is expected here.
> *¶12 #9 [moderate]:* “the drop of rain, which ___ another one”: joining/adhering is the key condition for a single great pearl rather than many small.
> *¶12 #10 [tentative]:* Keeps the logic: the drop retains the other droplet so the waters form one great commodity rather than dispersing.
> *¶12 #12 [tentative]:* Connects “form them in … and in a great, valued commodity”: “and thus” gives impersonal causal flow.

**¶13** “Behold, [I, I have] taught you how sea-pearls shall be formed. I have told you that as a pearl shall come into existence by means of rainwater that has [ti]me to become foam, the pearl-shell shall come into existence by means of the foam, and the foam itself comes into being by means of the transformation and the [stirring] of the sea.

> *¶13 #3 [moderate]:* Foam comes from transformation and the stirring/agitation of the sea; correspondence: disturbance at the boundary produces the first vessel (foam/shell).

**¶14** “Then immediately at the time when [the pearls are complete] the pearl divers know it, they shall [go down] and they [d]own to those places and they bring pearls up from the depths of the sea, and each pearl diver finds according to the fortune that is [ordai]ned for him. The pearl divers shall [gi]ve them to the traders, and the traders give them to the kings and the nobles.

> *¶14 #1 [moderate]:* Triggers the divers’ knowledge: when pearls are complete/ripe, they are sought. Substrate: natural maturity condition, not moral timing.
> *¶14 #2 [strong]:* Divers then “___ and they ___down”: first movement is to go down/enter the sea.

**¶16** “[The] booty that shall be seized is like the dr[op of r]ainwater, while the apostles are like the divers. The traders are the light-givers of the heavens; the kings and no[b]les are the aeons of greatness.

**¶17** “Fo[r a]ll the souls that ascend in the flesh of mankind and are freed shall be brought back to the great aeons of light. A place of rest comes about for them, at that place in the aeons of greatness.

**Assessment:** Most gaps can be restored at the level of process-logic (descent of the drop, surface-foam, receptacle/shell, joining vs fragmentation, maturation cue for divers). Where the manuscript likely carried specific adjectives or technical terms (¶9 #4–5; ¶12 #8–10; ¶14 #1), the correspondential constraints fix the function (receptacle, agitation, adhesion, completion) but not always the exact historical wording; those are therefore tentative/moderate.

---

## Chapter 84: Concerning Wisdom; it is far superior when on the Tongue than in the Heart of the Person.

**¶1** Behold, when a person will be taught wise wisdom in his heart, and he seals it in his doctrine, so shall you find him rejoicing greatly about it. However, [sh]all his [companions speak] to him, more th[an] when he may pro-c[claim it] and utter it. ⟨p.205⟩ He shall be enlightened by it, and [t]hat [wisdom] shall shine forth the more in him. It is un-veil[ed be]fore him, and through it he assumes power and truth.

> *¶1 #2 [moderate]:* Parallel to ¶13–16: wisdom rejoiced over by those akin/with Mind, not by strangers. Here the ‘sealed’ wisdom still acts inwardly: his own inner ‘companions’ (powers/affections) respond to it more than public speech does.
> *¶1 #4 [strong]:* Completes the verb phrase ‘proclaim … and utter it’.

**¶2** I understand [indeed] that this word I have uttered is correct. I know that the wis-dom I have been taught is spread through my heart and perfected in my soul. (However), it is not found like the splendour in me, so that I regard it [ad]vanced, except when I shall proclaim it by my mouth and utter it to others. Indeed, when I proclaim it, I am giving it to the ears [of] others to hear. Would I do these same things, even if I had never heard it in [my] days of being? Would I desire greatly and my heart be drawn to the wis-dom I now proclaim? [I entreat you]ou, my master, that you might instruct me as to [why] this wisdom becomes more advanced when I proclaim it, than when it is sealed in my heart.

> *¶2 #1 [moderate]:* Fits the speaker’s insistence/verification: ‘I understand indeed that this word… is correct.’
> *¶2 #6 [strong]:* Normalizes the editor’s ‘w/hy’ to the required word-form.

**¶3** Well [do] you ask! And great is this lesson for which you have sought, [that is] whence comes my great joy, on account of this wis-dom that I utter? [and you ask concerning why] it is superior in my mouth when I proclaim it than when it is set in m[y heart]. You yourself rejoice in it; and the other one who hears it from you, he shall rejoice in it, and be enlightened [by] it and receive thereby permanent strength.

> *¶3 #3 [moderate]:* Sets up the explicit question from ¶2 (‘why … more advanced when proclaimed’). Keeps impersonal-teaching register (causal inquiry) rather than exhortation.

**¶4** For like this matter, just so a small b[o]y who is conceived in the belly of his mother. He [and as] turns in his mother's womb, filling her womb. The mother knows and understands that this child she conceives is alive within her. She rejoices over him until [the tim]e when she gives birth. And he comes from her alive with his [limbs whole] and perfect in beauty, without defect, ⟨p.206⟩ [in] the living open air that is more sp[ac]ious than the first air he was in. He fills his eyes with the light and speaks with his living voice in the way of they who are born.

> *¶4 #2 [moderate]:* Makes the biology-simile coherent: ‘He lives and as he turns… filling her womb.’ (motion as sign of life).

**¶5** Now, the time when this woman conceives the child in her belly, her joy at conceiving him in her womb is not so [ve]ry great as when she gives birth and sees him; and is full of his [be]auty and stature in the space of a single moment. The love and joy over him shall be a hundred [t]imes greater than it was, now that she has given birth and seen him.

**¶6** For the first time when she conceived him in her belly, his beauty and the sight of his eyes was hidden from his m[o/th]er; but when she gave birth to him she saw his beauty. His s[t]ature and his loveliness came before the e[ye]s of his f[athe]r [a]nd his mother and all his relatives. They shall rejoic[e] over him more and more when they look upon him face to face and see his beauty and delightfulness.

**¶7** Just as in this simile, the wisdom that is present [in] the heart of the person is like the living child who is co[nceived] in the belly of his mother. And when he is taught and seals it in his heart, it becomes like the child who shall be born, and they see his beauty.

**¶8** So, in this way, the wisdom that the person proclaims, speak[ng] it from his heart, shall be advanced more and mor[e]. Its en-hancement and glory shall double from the time when the bea-uty and splendour of the saying will be displayed before the eyes [of t]hey who hear it, and it shall also advance for you [from what is outside] your hearing, and you are astonished at what you proclai[m].

> *¶8 #4 [moderate]:* Correspondentially: proclamation externalizes the internal form; the saying’s ‘beauty and splendour’ are displayed before others’ eyes/ears, and it also advances for the speaker through reception from without (reflexive confirmation).

**¶9** Once again, the wisdom is like this, while it is hidden in the heart of the person. [Bef]ore he has uttered it, it is just like [the blaze] of fire that is hidden in wood. An[d] that wood is [set aflame] by the blaze of the fire, but the garment of fir[e that exists in] the wood is not apparent. Indeed, you can see [many pieces of] ⟨p.207⟩ wood and they put them in a single house. It is impossible to [put li]ght to that house as long as the light within [the pieces of wood is hidden] until the time when they are added to the fire, and the light [comes f]orth from them. It is possible for that entire house [to be] lit by the light of a single piece of wood.

> *¶9 #6 [strong]:* The image requires plurality: you can see ‘many pieces of wood’ gathered, yet the inner fire is not manifest until joined to actual flame.
> *¶9 #8 [moderate]:* Matches the stated constraint: ‘as long as the light within …’ i.e., within the wood remains unmanifest.

**¶10** This is also the case with the wisdom that is in the heart of the person. [It] is like the fire that is hidden in the wood, as its light is not [d]isplayed. For its part, the wisdom is like this: its li[ght] is hidden and its glory is hidden in the heart; but when the person will proclaim it, its glory shall be displayed before the eyes and the ears of a multitude.

**¶13** For in this respect the wisdom is like this child about whom I have told you, the one who [was] born from the woman. Now, when he will be born, his father and mother and family circle shall rejoice over him. However, you find others grieving by reason of him, [s]ince they are strangers to him. These are not reckoned among his family. [They do not]ot rejoice over him, because he is not of their race.

**¶14** This is also [the case] with the wisdom. When it is proclaimed by the mouth of the teacher, these who are [a]kin to it shall receive it to them and rejoice in it; but [those] who are strangers to it neither rejoice in it [nor are they enlightened by it] nor receive it to them.

> *¶14 #4 [moderate]:* Balances the triad in the chapter: (1) rejoice, (2) receive, (3) be enlightened/strengthened. Strangers lack the inner kinship (¶13) / Mind (¶16), so the uttered wisdom does not become light in them.

**¶15** Just like the light of [the fire, which I] proclaim[ed] to you, that shall come from the wood [and be apparent out]utside before the eyes of every one. ⟨p.208⟩ So, [wh]oever looks shall see the light that has [come fro]m the wood; but whoever is blind does not see the [fir]e.

**¶16** This is also the case with the wisdom, when it will be pro-claimed. The person, in whom is the [Mi]nd, of him is the wisdom. Whenever he may hear it, he shall receive it in to him; but the one who has no Mind in him is a stranger to it. He neither receives it in to him, nor shall he listen to [it].

**Assessment:** Most gaps are short and mechanically constrained. The few substantial lacunae (¶1#2, ¶3#3, ¶4#2, ¶8#4, ¶9#6/8, ¶14#4) were restored by following the chapter’s correspondential structure: wisdom as internal fire/child, proclamation as birth/ignition, reception conditioned by kinship/Mind, and strangers’ non-reception expressed as lack of rejoicing/enlightenment.

---

## Chapter 85: Concerning the Cross of Light: [...] trample upon it.

**¶3** Now, when you walk on [your] way about some godly matter, as your commanding superior sends you, you do no sin therein. For you are not walking there about some lustful matter, nor concerning [some matter] of advantage, wherein is sin; but you make your walkway due to the work of God, as you hasten [and do you hasten] because of your own desire.

> *¶3 #2 [moderate]:* The pair modifies “concerning … of advantage, wherein is sin”; in the oldest register this is simply ‘a matter/thing of gain’, not a moralizing category. The remaining phrase already supplies “of advantage”.
> *¶3 #3 [moderate]:* The clause contrasts divine commission vs self-will: “you make your walkway due to the work of God, as you hasten … not because of your own desire.” The missing words must carry the finite verb that is then negated by “not … because of your own desire.”

**¶4** Indeed, I, I tell you with a great voice: Every elect and righteous person who walks on a path due to the work of God, and though walking upon the earth and though treading with the soles of his feet on the Cross of Light, he does no sin therein. Rather, a crown and palm-branch is his entire path; because he walks not by his own desire, nor hastens about an acquisition and worthless things, as he tramples upon the earth and the Cross of Light.

**¶5** However, the other person who hastens on the path because of his own lust and [the desire] of his body, as he [hastens] his own about an acquisition and [worthless] things [also]—a total sin is the walkway of that person, walking on the path of sin and error.

> *¶5 #1 [moderate]:* Parallel to ¶4 (“by his own desire”): here it is the bodily impulse that drives the motion.
> *¶5 #2 [moderate]:* Balances the earlier “hastens on the path”; the verb is required before “his own about an acquisition…”.
> *¶5 #3 [strong]:* Matches ¶4’s fixed pair “an acquisition and worthless things.”
> *¶5 #4 [tentative]:* The sequence requires a connective: he hastens about acquisition and worthless things also—therefore the whole walkway is sin.

**¶6** [Therefore] behold, I have told you: Any one who, if his teacher will send him, goes on a path about some godly matter, a total victory is his path. Still, you also recounted this: “I trample upon the earth and the Cross of Light, I oppress with my [sole].” As you say: “Perhaps I have therein sin because of [the de]ed.”

> *¶6 #1 [moderate]:* Resumes the argument and draws inference from ¶3–5: since commissioned walking is blameless, the speaker says “Therefore, behold, I have told you…”.

**¶7** Again, do not be afraid even with this; because the living soul is like this. As a person, ⟨p.210⟩ if a disease will take hold of his limbs, and his heart [is sl]ow, his soul distressed, and he has pain in his disease. He seeks after a doctor to cure him, to make him strong of heart. And he cures, attends to, and gives him health [from] the disease that has risen up against him. So he thinks about this.

**¶8** [And] a wise person comes, knowing the formulas proper to a skilled doctor. If he shall achieve a healing of the one whom he [shall need] it, he recites over him the formulas that he knows, and he even tramples with his foot upon all his limbs! That person, who is ill, knows that this person is trampling upon him so that he might achieve a healing; rather, not doing this to him out of some enmity he had against him beforehand. Instead, all that he does, he does to him that he might make a healing of his body. And he expels [the] disease and the pain, trampling upon him to occasion this benefit. That person, who is ill, he can bear no anger nor hatred against this doctor, who tramples upon him. He can not hate him out of enmity, because he knows that he does this to him to his benefit.

> *¶8 #2 [moderate]:* The construction: “If he shall achieve a healing of the one whom he … it” demands a verb of requirement/need: the doctor heals the one who ‘needs’ it.

**¶9** However, should someone come and trample upon him in enmity; he may hate, prevent and not permit him! For he knows that he tramples upon him in enmity; and as he strikes out of a wanton whim, he piles another illness upon his disease.

**¶12** Rather, the word that wounds is this: should a person utter a saying for the sake of the murder of a person, or the murder of animals, or for [the sake of] the murder of trees and the Cross of Light, the lying word and the wrathful and the bitter, or a word of envy and [malice] only, or a word of accusation, which someone will spread against his brother; this is the [wor]d that wounds [the soul]. Should the person utter it, it befits [si]n; because [he has br]ought forth from it the demons [and the archons] concerning this [and has set them in motion] the righteous person, or [word] all of them, which I have proclaimed, in order that [has wounded] his word to the fetter; because anyone who would [or else] his word to the fetter. And again, he [he may bind] his coming forth, they will bind him in the place wherein he has bound his [bind].

> *¶12 #2 [moderate]:* List of speech-impulses: lying, wrathful, bitter, envy—then another interior source. In the oldest substrate the opposite of healing speech is speech from inward ill-will/malice.
> *¶12 #4 [moderate]:* In this medical-correspondential context, wounding words wound the living soul (the Light bound in mixture), not merely a ‘person’s feelings’.
> *¶12 #7 [moderate]:* Oldest cosmological register pairs ‘demons’ with ruling powers/archons as the agencies stirred up by destructive speech.
> *¶12 #8 [tentative]:* What follows requires a causal chain from utterance → arousing hostile powers → binding by one’s own act. ‘Set in motion’ fits the impersonal causal style.
> *¶12 #9 [moderate]:* The demonstrative “this …” points back to the injurious utterance itself as the operative cause.
> *¶12 #10 [tentative]:* The sentence contrasts ‘righteous person, or … all of them’; the operative act of the word is to wound/harm.
> *¶12 #11 [tentative]:* Provides the alternative scope: against one righteous person, or else against all the classes previously named.
> *¶12 #12 [moderate]:* The logic is correspondential retribution: he ‘binds’ by his utterance; thus the text moves to “in order that … his word to the fetter”.
> *¶12 #13 [strong]:* The repeated motif is ‘word to the fetter’—speech that creates bonds; the verb must echo that: “anyone who would bind his word to the fetter.”

**¶14** [He is li]ke this, a wise doctor who might turn back [the violence of] his disease. As a wound is in his body, he cuts [the flesh around] this person who is ill, in his wisdom, [and burns] he [so that] this wound, and he cuts it. After these things [may open] and he places the soluble drugs upon it, so that [they will be able to diss]olve. He might incise it with the iron, and he casts it out ⟨p.212⟩ [f]rom him, with the soluble drugs. And the wound is purified [fr]om the fever. After these events, he shall place the soothing drugs [afterwards] and it heals up. That person, who is ill, he shall give [thanks] but he shall not [revile him] he [shall not] generate anger in his heart against him; because he knows that he is making the aspect of his benefit and his healing. After the cut that he may make, and the cauterizing that he may do there, he shall even give rewards to the doctor! He gives his thanks and is a friend to him his whole life through.

> *¶14 #2 [tentative]:* The wise doctor turns back not the person but the force/violence of the disease—an impersonal, causal phrasing consistent with the substrate.
> *¶14 #3 [tentative]:* A wound ‘in his body’ implies cutting away surrounding flesh/tissue to remove corruption; the image serves correspondence for removing falsity/poison from the outer level.
> *¶14 #4 [moderate]:* The next line mentions cauterizing; the missing clause likely introduced burning/cautery as part of the procedure.
> *¶14 #5 [moderate]:* Required to link action to purpose: “in his wisdom, … so that he … this wound.”
> *¶14 #6 [moderate]:* The doctor opens/exposes the wound in order to cut it out; this matches later “incise it with the iron.”
> *¶14 #10 [moderate]:* Sequence marker: after purifying and dissolving/casting out, he then applies soothing drugs and it heals.
> *¶14 #11 [tentative]:* Parallel to the end: “He gives his thanks”; earlier: “he shall give … but he shall not …”. ‘Thanks’ fits the expected grateful response.
> *¶14 #12 [moderate]:* Opposed to giving thanks: the patient does not revile/accuse the doctor despite pain—matching the earlier theme about accusation/words that wound.

**Assessment:** Most gaps are small and could be restored by tight parallelism (commissioned walking vs self-willed acquisition; the repeated pair ‘acquisition and worthless things’; the binding/fetter motif). The long damaged stretch in ¶12 required correspondential reconstruction: harmful speech as an efficient cause that ‘binds’ the speaker by stirring hostile powers, wounding the living soul. Several items there remain tentative because the syntax could permit alternate Coptic connective verbs, but the underlying causal structure is constrained.

---

## Chapter 86: The Chapter of the Man who asks: Why [am] I sometimes at peace, and troubled at other times?

**¶2** Sometimes [I] am peaceful in my heart, as my consideration [is constructive], and all my doctrines are ordered and in place. I conduct [without fault in] my works, as I utter my words to all people with sweetness. I am peaceful of heart in the gladness of the Mind. ⟨p.214⟩ Even my body is carefree, and my soul [rejo]ices in wisdom and true knowledge.

> *¶2 #2 [strong]:* Fits the correspondential opposition to ¶3 where “wicked considerations” disorder doctrines; here the inward “consideration” is formative/ordering.
> *¶2 #3 [moderate]:* The sequence demands an adverbial qualifier on the outward works matching the inward order (“doctrines … ordered and in place”). The oldest-layer register prefers an impersonal ethical-structural term (“without fault”) rather than later moralizing; it also balances ¶3 “I shall do … in my works” where the outward becomes disordered.

**¶3** There are also times when I shall be troubled. My doctrines are confused. Gloom increases with them, and grief and anger and envy and lust. I am troubled, struggling with all my might that I would subdue them; not finding the strength of mind to subdue them at that hour nor day when they surged up in me. And all these evil teachings and wicked considerations come forth. They have tormented me. I do not comprehend, because there is no single shape in all these counsels that have entered me. Are they revealed to me, or indeed did they enter me from outside and have been shaken into me? I do not know if they have wakened within me alone, have rebelled inside me and set themselves up against me. I do not know about that. However, this only I understand: that I shall be very badly confused in all my doctrines. I shall do [confusion] in my works, and you find my word is worthless in my mouth. I shall not find how [I may be rid of them], and I become a source of trouble and confusion for my brothers. Even my body rises up against me, and sickens for that entire day.

> *¶3 #1 [strong]:* Direct antithesis to ¶2/¶10: ordered doctrine → confusion; and it anticipates “source of trouble and confusion.”
> *¶3 #2 [moderate]:* The clause needs an infinitival complement after “I shall not find how …”: inability to expel/subdue the intruding counsels. This matches the earlier struggle “that I would subdue them; not finding the strength…”.

**¶6** Well do you ask about this lesson. Behold, I will explain it to you! ⟨p.215⟩ Understand this: The soul that assumes the body, when the Light Mind will come to it, shall be purified by the power of wisdom and obedience, and it is cleansed and made a new man. There is no trouble in (the soul), nor confusion nor disturbance.

**¶7** However, when a disturbance will arise for him and he will be troubled, this disturbance shall go into him in [many ways], first through his birth-signs and his difficult stars that [rule him—] they turn over him and stir him and trouble him with lust and anger and depression and grief, as he wills. Also, as he wills, the powers of heaven shall trouble him through their roots, to which he is attached.

> *¶7 #1 [strong]:* The disturbance is said to enter through multiple correspondential ‘channels’: stars/roots below/above, then later food/water. “Many ways” keeps the impersonal cosmological tone.
> *¶7 #2 [moderate]:* Syntax requires a finite verb before “they turn over him and stir him…”. In this substrate, stars ‘rule/turn’ a person by their dominion; “rule him” sets up the subsequent actions (turn, stir, trouble).

**¶8** Again, there are times you shall find the powers of heaven are peaceful and tranquil for him, and it comes to [calmness, and then] a difficult part comes into him by the nourishment that he has eaten, or else indeed in some [bread] or in the water that they have drunk. Again, trouble and confusion and anger will increase in him, and lust multiplies upon him together with depression and grief; because of the nourishment of the bread he has eaten and the water he has drunk, which are full of bothersome parts, a vengeful counsel. They shall enter his body, [mixed in] with these foods, and they even become joined in with the wicked parts of the body and the sin that is in him; transferring the anger and the lust and the depression and the grief, these wicked thoughts of the body.

> *¶8 #1 [moderate]:* The sentence contrasts a tranquil heavenly influx with a sudden intrusion from nourishment; “and then” introduces the reversal demanded by the correspondential logic.
> *¶8 #2 [strong]:* Parallelism with the next clause “or in the water that they have drunk” and later repetition “nourishment of the bread… and the water…”.
> *¶8 #3 [strong]:* Editor fill retained; it matches the ‘mixture’ doctrine: alien troublesome parts are carried in and commingled with bodily parts.

**¶10** There are times, however, if you shall find that the nourishment that comes into you is pure [and well refined], so it is greater in light and life while the waste is less in it, and the evil-doing is diminished from it. Even the atmosphere above is still, being purified through the [pure airs; and] also the image is calmed from [every agitation; and] increases in you through ⟨p.216⟩ those good parts of the food that come into you. And they find you at ease and tranquil, as you are well governed in your abodes because of the living part, which is greater on account of the light of these justified souls that are in it. The ones that have perfected their deeds. Their dues have ceased, while their soul is carefree [wisdom] they have been allied with these living souls that [are p]resent in you. Due to this you shall find them in you, calm in tranquillity, and they come from you without trouble; and you are found flourishing of body. Even your deeds are determined, being well constructed after their manner, and your doctrines are in order, as your words [and because] your soul is carefree within you, ascending like a bird.

> *¶10 #1 [moderate]:* Completes “pure …” with a second purity-term typical of the substrate’s material correspondences (refined/clarified = more Light, less residue).
> *¶10 #2 [moderate]:* The “atmosphere above” is calmed by purification of the airs/winds (a standard cosmological conduit). This keeps the upward/downward conduit schema used in ¶11.
> *¶10 #3 [moderate]:* The “image” (inner form) is calmed when stirred motions cease; “agitation” corresponds to the turbulent lust/anger listed elsewhere.
> *¶10 #4 [strong]:* What “increases in you through those good parts” is the ordered, luminous faculty named elsewhere: wisdom/true knowledge/Mind in gladness.
> *¶10 #6 [moderate]:* Bridges the causal explanation: carefree soul is so “because” of alliance with the living souls present within (light-parts in the pure nourishment).

**¶11** Behold, I have explained to you the determination of this lesson for which you asked: Whence come these bad counsels and considerations into you, these vengeful thoughts that trouble; and also whence come into you these good counsels and sweet doctrines, when your Mind is calmed for you. I have told you that when [your bo]dy is troubled, you shall be troubled by the conduits of they below, the difficult parts that come into you by food and the act of eating. Again, when the conduits above are calmed from you, even your food that has come into you is clear and pure. Your heart shall be found ordered in its place, your counsels at peace from disturbance.

**Assessment:** Most gaps are constrained by the chapter’s conduit-structure (above/below; stars/roots; food/water) and by the strict opposition between ordered/light states and confused/dark states. Where the Coptic leaves room (¶2 #3; ¶3 #2; ¶10 #1–3, #6), restorations are guided by the oldest-layer correspondential mechanics: inward order expresses itself as faultless outward work; disturbances enter by multiple channels; purification/refinement of nourishment and airs calms the inner image and increases wisdom.

---

## Chapter 87: Concerning the Alms, that [ ... ] life in the Church.

**¶5** What does the assembly of catechu[me]ns resemble? It is like good earth [that is made ready] in which the gardener shall plant a good tree. [Beho]ld, [th]at good tree transforms the power and the [strength / fertility] of all earth, and it produces good fruit. [Th]at [gardener] sets at ease the tree of life, and he r[ests himself].

> *¶5 #2 [moderate]:* The assembly is compared to “good earth” in a prepared state: a receptive natural plane made fit to receive influx/seed. The oldest substrate prefers an impersonal, process description (made ready/prepared) over exhortation.
> *¶5 #5 [moderate]:* Correspondentially the good tree (living formation of Light in a receptive ground) changes the earth’s potency so it can bear fruit: ‘power’ pairs naturally with a second term like fertility/fruitfulness rather than moral qualities. Kept as a balanced hendiadys.

**¶7** Likewise this [liv]ing sou[l] that today is set in mixture; for it wishes to [as]cend and go to the house of its people, but it kn[ow[s a]nd understands that it has no open door in all the powers of heaven and earth. For they are its oppression that [shuts] it everywhere. Indeed, it has no open door except the sun and the moon themselves, the light-givers of the heavens [and thus / therefore] they become the place of rest for it. And they become a door that opens for it [in] the coming forth, and (the soul) comes out through them to the country of the household of i[ts] people.

> *¶7 #5 [moderate]:* The powers of heaven and earth, in the mixed region, function as constraining ‘bindings’ on the soul; the clause needs a verb of closure/oppression: they ‘shut’ it in on every side.
> *¶7 #6 [moderate]:* A short inferential link: since only the sun and moon are open to it, ‘and thus/therefore’ they become its resting-place. This keeps the impersonal, structural reasoning of the substrate.

**¶8** Again, further in, also the light-givers of the heavens have no place of rest amongst all the powers of heaven; except for the lan[d] of light, that has indeed been theirs for ever.

**¶9** So, thus the living soul is purified of all afflictions; and they provide a rest for it, and open a door that opens to the house [of] its people.

**¶10** Also, it is the case that the land of lig[ht] will become the final receiver for the light-givers of [the] heavens, and they will rest themselves therein and rule in it [for] ever.

**Assessment:** Most gaps are trivial letter-restorations. The two larger restorations are constrained by the correspondential chain: receptivity/preparation of the ‘earth’ (¶5) and the structural oppression/closure by the mixed powers (¶7), plus a brief inferential connector (¶7). Exact Coptic lexeme behind these remains uncertain, but the restored sense matches the oldest cosmological substrate’s impersonal mechanics of influx, constraint, purification, and ascent.

---

## Chapter 88: Concerning the Catechumen who found fault with the Elect: why he is angry.

**¶5** This is also the case with the elect. They are gods as they stand firm in the image of the gods. The divinity that is planted in them came to them from the heights and [dw]elt in them.

**¶6** Further, when they will be troubled and angry and resentful, [they] curse each other. However, do not you be troubled in your heart. You know, once and for all, they are established in a body that is not their own, hating the Flesh of Sin that makes the other dwell in a foreign land. Due to this they shall be angry, and say and utter hard words to each others' faces.

**¶7** For you have known the mystery of the two essences, you have understood that what is good and what is evil dwell in every person. You have also understood that the saints bear a great burden on their shoulders. For you yourself have understood that they carry a great burden, they are established in the body that is not their own.

**¶8** Your hands are always free to beat the Cross of Light.

**¶11** For when quarrelling will arise among the saints [in the body] because the old man too dwells in their body; and due to this they shall take offence [at each other’s weakness,] they {dispute} with one another.

> *¶11 #1 [moderate]:* The quarrel is explained immediately by the presence of the “old man” dwelling in their body. In the oldest substrate the causal link is structural: contention arises *in/through the body* as the mixed vessel where contrary essences press against one another. Thus a short locative phrase that anticipates “because the old man too dwells in their body” fits the correspondential logic.
> *¶11 #2 [tentative]:* The sequence demands: offence → hardening/striking → dispute. In the substrate register, ‘taking offence’ is not moralizing but the reaction of the mixed state: the evil in the body seizes on what is infirm in the other’s outer person. A phrase like “at each other’s weakness,” coheres with the earlier theme (hard words to faces; foreign body; Flesh of Sin) and leads naturally into “they dispute with one another.”

**Assessment:** Two lacunae are trivial (letter/word) and are certain. The two gaps in ¶11 are restorably constrained by the causal structure (quarrel arises in the bodily vessel because the old man is present; offence leads into mutual dispute), but the exact Coptic translator’s wording cannot be recovered with certainty.

---

## Chapter 89: The Chapter of the Nazorean who questions the Teacher

**¶6** “The judge is not a man of wickedness, but rather the work counteracts wickedness, limiting the evil. Nevertheless, what you have recounted is that the judge shall go and punish, kill and slay. He acts like this that he might annul the evil and impede the wickedness in the person; and lay it upon the head of the sinner.

**¶11** “Nevertheless, God has not done anything that is ev[il to] them, rather they to themselves. It is their own deeds that shall con- demn them and cast them in the gehenna of burning.

**Assessment:** Single, mid-word lacuna; restoration is mechanically certain from the surrounding letters (“ev[il to] them” → “evil to them”). No further correspondential constraint needed.

---

## Chapter 90: Concerning the Fifteen Paths; and whether the Catechumen would have avoided causing his Wealth to go on the Three Paths to Gehenna

**¶2** For we have heard thus from you: ‘Fifteen paths are drawn out in the zone. Four paths are pure and belong [to the] light, leading up to life. Eight other paths are [mixed], leading above from that place. The light shall go [u]p and become free through them; be purified and go in [to the] ships. However, the waste is separated and thrown [dow]n to transmigr[a]tion and the other three paths of [waste are] discharged to the gehennas’.

> *¶2 #7 [moderate]:* The syntax requires a genitival qualifier: “the other three paths of waste are discharged…”. In the oldest cosmological register, these are not ‘sins’ but effluxes/residues; “are” supplies the needed verbal hinge before “discharged to the gehennas.”

**¶3** You have explained [that the three] paths that belong to waste, the ones that are drawn to the gehennas, [a]re drawn from the fleshes. O[n]e of them is the appetite for [lawl]essness, that of people and of all flesh. The second is the [heat in] all bodies, which is slaughter, [with] which all flesh is consumed. The third is the damaging action, which [strikes] the Cross of Light and every body; together with the rest of the error and the blasphemy that wounds the gods.

> *¶3 #5 [tentative]:* The second waste-path must be a universal, non-moral consumption-principle that ‘(slaughter) consumes all flesh’. In the Iranian correspondential scheme, devouring/combustion corresponds to the hot ferocity of the lower powers; thus “the heat in all bodies, which is slaughter” keeps the impersonal cosmological tone and explains the mechanism by which bodies become food.
> *¶3 #7 [moderate]:* The third path is “damaging action” and must take a transitive verb governing “the Cross of Light and every body”. “Strikes” fits the correspondential sense: violent impact that wounds the axial bond (Cross of Light) and its ultimates (bodies), aligning with “error and blasphemy that wounds the gods.”

**¶4** Th[ese th]ree paths have you taught us, that they lead to the gehennas. And you have also [told] us that every person shall follow after hi[s] de[e]ds, whether to life or indeed to death.

**Assessment:** Most lacunae are trivial letter/word restorations constrained by fixed surrounding text. The two substantial gaps in ¶2 #7 and ¶3 #5 required correspondential completion: (1) marking the three remaining paths as waste being discharged downward, and (2) identifying a cosmological consumption-principle in bodies that manifests as slaughter. The verb in ¶3 #7 is tightly constrained by the need to govern ‘Cross of Light and every body’ and to parallel ‘wounds the gods’.

---

## Chapter 91: Also concerning the Catechumen; shall he be saved in a single Body?

**¶3** The sign of that perfect catechumen is this: You shall find the woman in the house with him, and beside him she is like these strangers. His house, in his reckoning, shall be like these lodging houses. He says: I am living in a house for rent by days and months. His brothers and his relatives shall be, in his reckoning, necessary as foreign people who take up with him, while travelling on the road with him. He [knows that] they will separate from him, and each one [day] may [not] the gold and the silver and the utensils of the [rental] house. To him, they shall be like borrowed vessels; [he] takes them, is served by them, and afterwards gives them to their owner. He does not set his trust on them, nor his treasure.

> *¶3 #1 [moderate]:* The sequence contrasts temporary companionship with inevitable separation. The substrate register favors an impersonal gnomic turn (“he knows that … they will separate”).
> *¶3 #2 [moderate]:* Travel-companions separate “each one (goes) to his own road/day.” The immediate context is lodging “by days and months,” so “day” is the natural term for each one’s allotted segment/time.
